# EXPANSION CW125-01 — The Price of Trust

## A prose-first memorial expansion about the remembered claim, the people who inherit it, and the blanks that remain human.

### Prose Wave 125: What a Warning Cannot Finish

## Batch brief

**Content type:** original game-content proposal with full scene, attributable writing, conversation, and conditional-return drafts.
**Content bank:** 48 beat pairings; four alternative authored forms for each pairing; 192 candidates, of which a verified consumer should select only what it can support.
**Current local anchor:** memorial_black_flotilla_victim/price_of_trust — Black Flotilla Victim.
**Tone:** A memorial that converts one survivor’s reported betrayal into a warning about trust, and the uneasy reader who refuses to let a warning become a universal law.
**Scope:** narrative prose only; no production code, authoritative JSON edits, mechanics, route, quest, flag, simulation, or save change.

## 1. Expansion thesis

This plan explores A memorial that converts one survivor’s reported betrayal into a warning about trust, and the uneasy reader who refuses to let a warning become a universal law. It uses the source text “A survivor who fell victim to the Black Flotilla's treachery. They trusted, they traded, they died. Remember: trust is a luxury we cannot afford.” as a narrow canon anchor, not as an excuse to manufacture witnesses or explain every silence. Six movements and forty-eight beat pairings explore how different present readers may respond to the wording. The proposed passages remain optional editorial drafts until a current owner and route are verified.

## 2. Story question

How can a reader take the recorded betrayal seriously without allowing one public sentence to decide what every later person is permitted to risk?

## 5. Human center

The tension between a particular memorial claim and the final sentence’s attempt to make caution sound universal.
 The deceased or collective subject must never become a device whose only purpose is to teach a clean lesson to the living. Keep the person or people larger than the inscription’s most repeatable line.

## 6. Voice and point of view

- **A reader who has relied on others:** Speaks concretely about dependence without using personal experience to disprove the memorial.
- **A keeper of public words:** Wants the warning preserved but knows a memorial is not a complete archive.
- **A younger listener:** Asks whether the final sentence protects people or narrows what they can imagine.

The people listed here are proposed present-day readers, not canon characters and not witnesses unless another verified source establishes that history. Keep the original memorial voice distinct from any reply written for this plan.

## 8. Player agency

The player may read, ask who authored a claim, hear a second interpretation, decline to discuss it, or leave without a verdict. These are editorial postures rather than promised controls. Silence must remain a complete response. Do not attach approval, trust, reputation, item, resource, treatment, memorial completion, or relationship state to the reader’s interpretation.

## 9. Continuity, dignity, and safety

Do not invent the victim’s name, relationship, trade, bargain, or final moments; no exact Flotilla history, terms of exchange, motive, battle, or faction doctrine is established. Keep the sentence “trust is a luxury” attributable to this memorial’s public voice. Do not turn caution into a universal relationship rule, a trade mechanic, a morality score, or advice about real people.
 Keep the source statement, a proposed speaker’s interpretation, and any verified world fact visibly separate. Avoid reenacting death, assigning fault without evidence, or converting grief into a tutorial.

## 11. Narrative sequence

These six editorial movements organize the prose bank; they are not six required visits, a chronology, or a quest chain. A verified consumer may use a single beat, a short excerpt, or none of the candidates.

### Movement 1: The Warning Arrives First

The reader meets a conclusion before learning the shape of the loss that produced it. In this movement the candidate question is: What does a warning let a reader know, and what does it leave sealed? The memorial’s phrase “a survivor” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 2: Trust Before the Trade

The two verbs “trusted” and “traded” sit close together but do not explain a bargain. In this movement the candidate question is: Can exchange be remembered without inventing its terms? The memorial’s phrase “Black Flotilla” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 3: The Unnamed Person

The public line gives the victim a description but withholds a name. In this movement the candidate question is: What care is owed to someone the record cannot identify? The memorial’s phrase “treachery” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 4: The Sentence About Luxury

The warning grows larger than the life it claims to remember. In this movement the candidate question is: Who is protected when a private conclusion is repeated as a rule? The memorial’s phrase “trusted” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 5: A Later Reader Objects

Another person needs connection even while honoring the warning. In this movement the candidate question is: Can disagreement preserve grief rather than dismiss it? The memorial’s phrase “traded” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 6: What Remains Unsettled

The ending keeps both caution and the need for trust in the same room. In this movement the candidate question is: What can a memorial remember without making its answer permanent? The memorial’s phrase “died” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

## 12. Beat bank and alternative passage drafts

Every movement meets each of eight source phrases. The four forms per pairing are alternatives: an observable scene, an attributable reader-authored fragment, a two-voice conversation, and a conditional return. Their headings help writers compare form; they do not promise that all versions fit one consumer. Dialogue is deliberately from proposed present readers, not from the person memorialized.

### Beat 01: The Warning Arrives First × a survivor

**Beat question:** When a reader meets “a survivor” during “The Warning Arrives First,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that the label gives the subject a status but not a biography?

#### Scene draft 001 — a survivor — The Warning Arrives First

This version starts in the small interval between recognizing the record and deciding what to repeat from it. The dramatic work of “The Warning Arrives First” comes from placing “a survivor” beside what the memorial does not say. No invented detail is allowed to close that gap. The proposed reader is not a witness to the life being memorialized; their presence only gives the source wording a present audience.

Let the focus settle on “a survivor”. The phrase has force because the label gives the subject a status but not a biography. A second person notices that force before anyone turns it into an explanation. They may look toward the line, then toward the person who has stopped reading, giving the beat a human action rather than an invented flashback.

A listener who is wary of turning the warning into a law begins with a modest response: “I can read a survivor, but I cannot tell from this sentence everything that happened. I want to keep the wording because it matters to someone, and I want to leave room where the record leaves room.” The line is written as present interpretation, not a quotation falsely attributed to the person on the memorial.

A quiet witness concerned with the unnamed person replies, “Then let us ask what it asks of us now. When a reader meets “a survivor” during “The Warning Arrives First,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that the label gives the subject a status but not a biography? I can honor the claim and still notice what it does not tell us.” The answer should be specific to the source phrase; it must not flatten the disagreement into a lesson about all grief or all survivors.

A third voice, a person remembering a promise they once depended on, may interrupt gently. They do not correct the catalog or add evidence. They ask whether the word “a survivor” is being repeated because it comforts the room, because it warns the room, or because no one knows what else to say. Let more than one motive remain possible.

The scene’s emotional turn around “a survivor” in “The Warning Arrives First” is small: one listener stops trying to finish the missing story for a moment. A chair shifts, a copied line is turned face down and then face up again, or the speaker chooses a less certain verb. The selected gesture should fit the verified presentation context; no physical memorial site is established by this proposal.

For “a survivor” in “The Warning Arrives First,” player-facing tone stays open. The player may read, ask for the source wording again, decline the conversation, or simply listen. These are writing possibilities, not a promised menu or branching system. No answer earns approval, trust, resources, safety, reputation, or a new relationship state.

The closing breath belongs to the living reader; do not fabricate an answer on behalf of the person commemorated. The last attention returns to “a survivor”, now heard through the live disagreement rather than treated as a complete biography.

**Drafting focus.** Keep the reader’s attention on “a survivor” rather than on a newly invented biography. The writer may ask When a reader meets “a survivor” during “The Warning Arrives First,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that the label gives the subject a status but not a biography? The answer should emerge through voice, hesitation, and what the speakers decline to claim; it should not be delivered by an omniscient narrator. This restraint is part of the content, not a substitute for dramatic movement.

**Voice and performance.** Let a listener who is wary of turning the warning into a law carry a rhythm distinct from the other proposed speakers. For this “The Warning Arrives First” beat on “a survivor,” that person can pause, qualify a sentence, or disagree with a word. They cannot borrow the deceased person’s interior voice or pretend that a catalog label gives them access to a private memory. Shape the delivery so the audience can hear the difference between a quoted source fact and an editorial response.

## 13. Player-facing tone

Keep sentences close to the source wording and the present reader’s actual knowledge. Allow one voice to value the memorial and another to object to its universal-sounding conclusion. Neither reaction should be framed as the correct morality. Use quiet material action rather than explanatory narration. Avoid turning illness, hunger, conflict, radiation, cold, machinery, duty, or trust into a puzzle tutorial or instruction to the player.

## 14. Editorial acceptance

Do not invent the victim’s name, relationship, trade, bargain, or final moments; no exact Flotilla history, terms of exchange, motive, battle, or faction doctrine is established. Keep the sentence “trust is a luxury” attributable to this memorial’s public voice. Do not turn caution into a universal relationship rule, a trade mechanic, a morality score, or advice about real people.
 Re-check the exact local record and any newer narrative data before selecting text. Retain only passages that the current consumer can reach and attribute honestly. Shortening a candidate is preferable to implying unsupported canon.

## 15. Character and relationship continuity

The proposed roles are anonymous present-day readers. Do not promote them into named survivors, historical witnesses, patients, faction leaders, victims, or permanent companions without a separate source and authorized content decision. Their relationships exist only within the selected text. Gratitude, skepticism, grief, forgiveness, caution, and disagreement do not become saved relationship values.

## 16. Passage-selection map

**Use a scene** when a verified reader-facing owner can stage a present response without claiming a sourced location. **Use a record** when existing content supports authorship and provenance. **Use a conversation** when a second perspective adds a genuine question rather than repeating the thesis. **Use a return vignette** only if the existing route already supports the callback condition; otherwise use its non-conditional final line or omit it. These are writing choices, not feature requests.
